They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   112→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   113→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   114→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   115→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   116→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   117→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   118→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   119→\n   120→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   121→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   122→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   123→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   124→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   125→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   126→\n   127→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   128→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   129→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   130→\n   131→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   132→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   133→\n   134→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   135→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   136→\n   137→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   138→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   139→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   140→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   141→\n   142→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   143→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   144→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   145→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   146→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   147→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   148→\n   149→## Decoder Reference\n   150→\n   151→- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   152→- Optional arguments:\n   153→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   154→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   155→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   156→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   157→\n   158→### Purpose\n   159→\n   160→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   161→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   162→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   163→3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   164→4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n   165→5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   166→\n   167→### Consistency requirements\n   168→\n   169→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\n   170→- Loading of data\n   171→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   172→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   173→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   174→\n   175→To check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n   176→\n   177→Whenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n   178→\n   179→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n   180→\n   181→Carefully verify your work after every step. description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nTo check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n\nWhenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above. \n\nCarefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \n\nYou will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference papers and code and these instructions. During testing, the reference version of `train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \n\n## Success Criteria\n\nA successful conversion should:\n1. Match the target data structure exactly\n2. Preserve all relevant information from the source\n3. Have consistent dimensions across trials/sessions\n4. Match information provided in the reference texts\n5. Include complete and accurate metadata\n6. Be reproducible with documented code\n7. All required files must be created:\n  - `CONVERSION_NOTES.md`\n  - `convert_data.py`\n  - `converted_data.pkl`\n  - `sample_data.pkl`\n  - `README.md`\n  - `train_decoder_full_out.txt`\n  - `conversion_sample_out.txt`\n  - `verification_sample_out.txt`\n  - `train_decoder_sample_out.txt`\n  - `conversion_full_out.txt`\n  - `verification_full_out.txt`\n8. **Pass all validation checks**:\n  - Data summary shows correct structure\n  - Visual inspection reveals no artifacts\n  - Dataset size and distribution statistics match reference texts\n  - Decoder achieves good accuracy\n  - Any validation failures are investigated and resolved\n9. a single unit in a single trial.\n   251→\n   252→    Args:\n   253→        spike_times: array of spike times (absolute)\n   254→        go_cue_time: go cue time (absolute)\n   255→        bin_edges: 261→    rel_times = spike_times - go_cue_time\n   262→    # Only consider spikes in the window\n   263→    mask = (rel_times >= bin_edges[0]) & (rel_times < bin_edges[-1])\n   264→    rel_times = rel_times[mask]\n   265→    # Count spikes in each bin\n   266→    counts, _ = np.histogram(rel_times, bins=bin_edges)\n   267→    # Convert to firing rates\n   268→    bin_width = bin_edges[1] - bin_edges[0]\n   269→    return counts.astype(np.float64) / bin_width\n   270→\n   271→\n   272→def get_tone_onset_relative_to_go(sample_start_times, go_cue_time):\n   273→    \"\"\"Find the tone 282→\n   283→\n   284→def extract_tongue_y_for_trial(tongue_timestamps, tongue_y,\n   285→                                go_cue_time, bin_edges):\n   286→    \"\"\"Extract tongue y-position in time     # Trial start times (needed for photostim onset conversion)\n   355→        trial_start_times = f['intervals/trials/start_time'][:]\n   356→\n   357→        # Sample start times (for tone onset                          for c in f['units/classification'][:]])\n   365→        anno_names = np.array([a.decode() if isinstance(a, bytes) else a\n   366→                              for a in 376→                continue\n   377→            region = map_anno_name_to_region(anno_names[i])\n   378→            if region is not None:\n   379→                unit_regions.append(region)\n   380→                unit_indices.append(i)\n   381→\n   382→        if len(unit_indices) == 0:\n   383→            if verbose:\n   384→                print(f\"  Skipping: no good units with valid        obs_intervals = f['units/obs_intervals'][:]\n   394→        obs_intervals_index = f['units/obs_intervals_index'][:]\n   395→\n   396→        # Find the minimum observation end time across all selected units\n   397→        min_obs_end = np.inf\n   398→        max_obs_start = -np.inf\n   399→        for ui in unit_indices:\n   400→            oi_start = 0 if ui == 0 else         min_obs_end = min(min_obs_end, unit_obs[-1, 1])\n   405→                max_obs_start = max(max_obs_start, unit_obs[0, 0])\n   406→\n   407→        # Only include trials where the go cue (and surrounding window) falls\n   408→        # within the recording observation window\n   409→        recording_valid = ((go_cue_times + T_START) >= max_obs_start - 0.1) & \\\n   410→                          ((go_cue_times + T_END) <= min_obs_end + 0.1)\n   411→\n   412→        if verbose and np.sum(~recording_valid) > 0:\n   413→            print(f\"  Recording window limits: {np.sum(recording_valid)}/{n_trials} trials have neural data\")\n   414→\n   415→        # === Session filtering ===\n   416→        # Use ALL trials for session-level performance calculation (as in the paper)\n   417→        # Control trials: no photostim, no early lick, no auto_water, no free_water\n   418→        is_control = ((early_lick == 'no early') &\n   419→ compute_firing_rates(spike_times, go_cue_time, bin_edges):\n    \"\"\"Compute firing rates for a single unit in a single trial.\n\n    Args:\n        spike_times: array of spike times (absolute)\n        go_cue_time: go cue time (absolute)\n        bin_edges: bin edges relative to go cue\n\n    Returns:\n        firing_rates: array of firing rates (Hz), shape (n_bins,)\n    \"\"\"\n    # Align spike times to go cue\n    rel_times = spike_times - go_cue_time\n    # Only consider spikes in the window\n    mask = (rel_times >= bin_edges[0]) & (rel_times < bin_edges[-1])\n    rel_times = rel_times[mask]\n    # Count spikes in each bin\n    counts, _ = np.histogram(rel_times, bins=bin_edges)\n    # Convert to firing rates\n    bin_width = bin_edges[1] - bin_edges[0]\n    return counts.astype(np.float64) / bin_width\n\n\ndef get_tone_onset_relative_to_go(sample_start_times, go_cue_time):\n    \"\"\"Find the tone onset time relative to go cue for a single trial.\n\n    The tone onset is the last sample_start before the go cue\n    (accounts for early lick replays).\n    \"\"\"\n    before = sample_start_times[sample_start_times < go_cue_time]\n    if len(before) > 0:\n        return before[-1] - go_cue_time  # negative value\n    return None\n\n\ndef extract_tongue_y_for_trial(tongue_timestamps, tongue_y,\n                                go_cue_time, bin_edges):\n    \"\"\"Extract tongue y-position in time bins for a single trial.\n\n    Uses all tongue tracking data (no confidence filtering) to compute\n    mean y-position per bin. NaN for bins with no tracking data.\n\n    Returns:\n        tongue_y_binned: array of mean y-positions per bin, shape (n_bins,)\n    \"\"\"\n    n_bins = len(bin_edges) - 1\n    tongue_y_binned = np.full(n_bins, np.nan)\n\n    # Get absolute time window\n    t_abs_start = go_cue_time + bin_edges[0]\n    t_abs_end = go_cue_time + bin_edges[-1]\n\n    # Find tongue data in this window\n    idx = np.searchsorted(tongue_timestamps, [t_abs_start, t_abs_end])\n    ts_window = tongue_timestamps[idx[0]:idx[1]]\n    y_window = tongue_y[idx[0]:idx[1]]\n\n    if len(ts_window) == 0:\n        return tongue_y_binned\n\n    # Align to go cue\n    ts_rel = ts_window - go_cue_time\n\n    # For each bin, compute mean y\n    for b in range(n_bins):\n        bin_mask = (ts_rel >= bin_edges[b]) & (ts_rel < bin_edges[b + 1])\n        if np.any(bin_mask):\n            tongue_y_binned[b] = np.mean(y_window[bin_mask])\n\n    return tongue_y_binned\n\n\ndef process_session(nwb_path, verbose=True):\n    \"\"\"Process a single NWB session file.\n\n    Returns:\n        session_data: dict with neural, input, output, metadata for this session,\n                      or None if session doesn't meet criteria\n    \"\"\"\n    if verbose:\n        print(f\"Processing: {os.path.basename(nwb_path)}\")\n\n    f = h5py.File(nwb_path, 'r')\n\n    try:\n        # Photostim info from trial table\n        ps_onset_str = np.array([p.decode() if isinstance(p, bytes) else p\n                                for p in f['intervals/trials/photostim_onset'][:]])\n        ps_dur_str = np.array([p.decode() if isinstance(p, bytes) else p\n                              for p in f['intervals/trials/photostim_duration'][:]])\n\n        # Go cue times\n        go_cue_times = f['acquisition/BehavioralEvents/go_start_times/timestamps'][:]\n\n        # Trial start times (needed for photostim onset conversion)\n        trial_start_times = f['intervals/trials/start_time'][:]\n\n        # Sample start times (for tone onset calculation)\n        sample_start_times = f['acquisition/BehavioralEvents/sample_start_times/timestamps'][:]\n\n        assert len(go_cue_times) == n_trials, f\"Go cue count ({len(go_cue_times)}) != trial count ({n_trials})\"\n\n        # === Load units ===\n        classification = np.array([c.decode() if isinstance(c, bytes) else c\n                                   for c in f['units/classification'][:]])\n        anno_names = np.array([a.decode() if isinstance(a, bytes) else a\n                              for a in f['units/anno_name'][:]])\n\n        # Filter by quality (classifier-based QC)\n        good_mask = classification == 'good'\n\n        # Map brain regions and filter units with valid regions\n        unit_regions = []\n        unit_indices = []\n        for i in range(len(classification)):\n            if not good_mask[i]:\n                continue\n            region = map_anno_name_to_region(anno_names[i])\n            if region is not None:\n                unit_regions.append(region)\n                unit_indices.append(i)\n\n        if len(unit_indices) == 0:\n            if verbose:\n                print(f\"  Skipping: no good units with valid regions\")\n            return None\n\n        unit_indices = np.array(unit_indices)\n        n_units = len(unit_indices)\n\n        # === Determine valid trials based on recording observation window ===\n        # Units may only have data for a subset of trials if the recording\n        # didn't span the entire behavioral session\n        obs_intervals = f['units/obs_intervals'][:]\n        obs_intervals_index = f['units/obs_intervals_index'][:]\n\n        # Find the minimum observation end time across all selected units\n        min_obs_end = np.inf\n        max_obs_start = -np.inf\n        for ui in unit_indices:\n            oi_start = 0 if ui == 0 else obs_intervals_index[ui - 1]\n            oi_end = obs_intervals_index[ui]\n            unit_obs = obs_intervals[oi_start:oi_end]\n            if len(unit_obs) > 0:\n                min_obs_end = min(min_obs_end, unit_obs[-1, 1])\n                max_obs_start = max(max_obs_start, unit_obs[0, 0])\n\n        # Only include trials where the go cue (and surrounding window) falls\n        # within the recording observation window\n        recording_valid = ((go_cue_times + T_START) >= max_obs_start - 0.1) & \\\n                          ((go_cue_times + T_END) <= min_obs_end + 0.1)\n\n        if verbose and np.sum(~recording_valid) > 0:\n            print(f\"  Recording window limits: {np.sum(recording_valid)}/{n_trials} trials have neural data\")\n\n        # === Session filtering ===\n        # Use ALL trials for session-level performance calculation (as in the paper)\n        # Control trials: no photostim, no early lick, no auto_water, no free_water\n        is_control = ((early_lick == 'no early') &\n                      (auto_water == 0) &\n                      (free_water == 0) &\n                      (ps_onset_str == 'N/A'))\n\n        control_hits_left = np.sum(is_control & (outcomes == 'hit') & (instructions == 'left'))\n        control_hits_right = np.sum(is_control & (outcomes == 'hit') & (instructions == 'right'))\n\n        # Performance on control trials (excluding ignore/no-response)\n        control_responding = is_control & (outcomes != 'ignore')\n        n_control_responding = np.sum(control_responding)\n        if n_control_responding == 0:\n            if verbose:\n                print(f\"  Skipping: no responding control trials\")\n            return None\n\n        performance = np.sum(is_control & (outcomes == 'hit')) / n_control_responding\n\n        if verbose:\n            print(f\"  Performance: {performance:.1%}, Correct L/R: {control_hits_left}/{control_hits_right}\")\n\n        if performance <= MIN_PERFORMANCE:\n            if verbose:\n                print(f\"  Skipping: performance {performance:.1%} <= {MIN_PERFORMANCE:.0%}\")\n            return None\n\n        if control_hits_left < MIN_CORRECT_PER_DIRECTION or control_hits_right < MIN_CORRECT_PER_DIRECTION:\n            if verbose:\n                print(f\"  Skipping: insufficient correct trials (L={control_hits_left}, R={control_hits_right})\")\n            return None\n\n        # --- Input: time from tone onset ---\n            tone_onset_rel = get_tone_onset_relative_to_go(sample_start_times, gc)\n            if tone_onset_rel is None:\n                # Fallback: use typical value\n                tone_onset_rel = -1.85\n            time_from_tone = BIN_CENTERS - tone_onset_rel  # time since tone onset at each bin\n\n            # --- Input: photostim on/off ---\n            photostim_binary = np.zeros(N_BINS, dtype=np.float64)\n            if ps_onset_str[ti] != 'N/A':\n                try:\n                    ps_onset_val = float(ps_onset_str[ti])\n                    ps_dur = float(ps_dur_str[ti])\n                    # Mark bins where photostim is on\n                    photostim_binary = ((BIN_CENTERS >= ps_start_rel) &\n                                       (BIN_CENTERS < ps_end_rel)).astype(np.float64)\n                except (ValueError, TypeError):\n                    pass\n\n            input_trial = np.stack([time_from_tone, photostim_binary], axis=0)  # Conversion Notes\n\n## Overview\n\nConverted NWB data from the brain-wide neural activity dataset to the standardized decoder format. The source data is from \"Brain-wide neural activity underlying memory-guided movement\" and analyzed in \"Brain-wide analysis reveals movement encoding structured across and within brain areas\".\n\n## Data Loading\n\n### Source Format\n- 174 NWB files across 28 subjects in `data/sub-*/sub-*.nwb`\n- Each NWB file represents one recording session\n- Some sessions have multiple probe insertions covering different subsets of trials\n\n### Spike Times\n- Loaded from `/units/spike_times` (ragged array with index in `/units/spike_times_index`)\n- Observation windows checked via `/units/obs_intervals` to determine which trials have valid neural data\n\n### Trial Data\n- Loaded from `/intervals/trials/` table\n- Go cue times from `/acquisition/BehavioralEvents/go_start_times/timestamps`\n- Sample start times from `/acquisition/BehavioralEvents/sample_start_times/timestamps`\n- Trial start times from `/intervals/trials/start_time`\n\n### Tongue Tracking\n- Side-view camera tracking from `/acquisition/BehavioralTimeSeries/Camera0_side_TongueTracking`\n- Shape (N, 3): [x, y, likelihood] from DeepLabCut\n- All tracking data used (no confidence filtering) for percentile computation and discretization\n\n## Processing Decisions\n\n### Neuron Quality Control\n- **Method**: Classifier-based QC (`classification='good'` in NWB units table)\n- **Matches reference**: The reference code uses `qc_mode='classifier'` which applies trained logistic regression classifiers per brain area\n- **Result**: 57,925 good units with valid brain regions across 144 sessions\n- **Paper reports**: 69,943 good units across 173 sessions (our count is lower due to session filtering)\n\n### Brain Region Mapping\n- NWB `anno_name` field contains detailed Allen Brain Atlas annotation names (293 unique)\n- Mapped to 14 high-level categories matching the reference code: ALM, OtherCortex, Orbital, Striatum, Pallidum, Thalamus, Hypothalamus, Hippocampus, Midbrain, Pons, Medulla, Cerebellum, Olfactory, CorticalSubplate\n- ALM = \"Secondary motor area\" (all layers) - the primary target region for this study\n- Units with empty or unmappable annotation names excluded (490 units, <1%)\n- Pretectal nuclei and Fields of Forel mapped to Thalamus following Allen Brain Atlas hierarchy\n\n### Session Filtering\n- **Performance threshold**: >65% correct on control trials (no photostim, no early lick, no auto_water, no free_water)\n- **Minimum correct trials**: >=50 correct left AND >=50 correct right trials\n- **Matches reference**: Methods text states \"overall behavioral performance (> 65%), and at least 50 correct lick left and lick right trials each\"\n- **Result**: 144 sessions kept, 30 skipped\n- **Skipped reasons**: 20 for low performance, 7 for insufficient correct trials per direction, 1 for no good units with valid regions, 2 for other reasons\n\n### Trial Filtering\n- Excluded `auto_water` and `free_water` trials (as in reference code)\n- **Kept** early lick trials (needed for early_lick decoder output)\n- **Kept** ignore/no-response trials (needed for outcome decoder output)\n- **Kept** photostimulation trials (needed for photostim decoder input)\n- Only included trials within the recording observation window (some NWB files contain trials beyond the neural recording period)\n\n### Temporal Alignment\n- Aligned to go cue onset (t=0)\n- Window: -2.5s to +1.5s (as specified in decoder task)\n- Reference code uses -3.0 to +3.0s or +3.5s, but decoder task specifies -2.5 to +1.5s\n\n### Firing Rate Computation\n- 50ms non-overlapping bins (as specified in decoder task)\n- Spike counts divided by bin width (0.05s) to get rates in Hz\n- 80 time bins per trial\n- Reference code uses 100ms bandwidth with 50ms stride (Gaussian-weighted), but decoder task specifies 50ms bins\n\n### Input Variables\n\n#### Time from tone onset\n- Computed as seconds since the first tone of the (successful) sample epoch\n- For trials with early lick replays, uses the last sample_start before the go cue\n- Typical value at go cue: ~1.85s (0.65s sample + 1.2s delay)\n- Range varies across trials due to early lick replays that extend the sample/delay epochs\n\n#### Photostimulation\n- Binary time series: 1 when photostim is on, 0 otherwise\n- `photostim_onset` in NWB is relative to trial start (converted to absolute then to go-cue-relative)\n- Photostim typically occurs during late delay epoch (-1.2s to -0.7s relative to go cue)\n- Duration: 0.5s (last 0.5s of delay including 100ms ramp-down)\n\n### Output Variables\n\n#### Choice (lick direction)\n- 0 = left, 1 = right\n- Based on `trial_instruction` field (which port the animal should lick)\n- Per-trial, broadcast across time bins\n\n#### Outcome\n- 0 = ignore (no response), 1 = miss (wrong port), 2 = hit (correct)\n- Per-trial, broadcast across time bins\n\n#### Early Lick\n- 0 = no early lick, 1 = early lick during sample/delay\n- Per-trial, broadcast across time bins\n\n#### Tongue Y-Position\n- Time-varying, discretized per session\n- 0 = below 40th percentile, 1 = 40th-60th percentile, 2 =           \"content\": \"1→Behavior and video tracking\\n     2→\\n     3→Mice performed an auditory delayed response task 21 (Figure 1A). The instruction stimuli during the sample epoch were pure tones played at one of two frequencies (3 kHz or 12 kHz). Each tone was played three times for 150 ms with 100 ms inter-tone intervals. The sample epoch was followed by a 1.2 s delay epoch. An auditory ‘Go’ cue (carrier frequency of 6 kHz with 360 Hz modulating fre-\\n     4→quency, 0.1 s duration) indicated the end of the delay epoch. Licking early during the sample/delay epoch triggered a replay of the epoch. During the response epoch (answer period: 1.5 s) mice reported the instruction by licking one of the two lick ports. Licking (consumption period: 1.5 s) the correct lick port triggered a small water reward (0.1 ~ 0.2 ul). Licking the incorrect lick port triggered a timeout (1-3 s). After mice stopped licking for 1.5 s, the trial ended, followed by a 250 ms inter-trial-interval. Early lick trials and no\\n     5→response trials were excluded for analysis. Overall performance was computed as the fraction of correct control trials (i.e. no photostimulation), excluding any early lick trials. We selected experimental sessions for analysis based on following criteria: overall behavioral performance (> 65%), and at least 50 correct lick left and lick right trials each. Mice performed on average 476 (Mean; range, 130-785) trials per session with 84% correct rate (range, 65-99%; STAR Methods). During the delay epoch, mice maintain a memory of their choice and plan a movement.\\n     6→\\n     7→Two CMOS cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) were used to track orofacial movements of the mouse under IR light illumination (940 nm LED). The cameras were equipped with 4-12 mm focal length lenses (12VM412ASIR, Tamron) and a pixel resolution of 71 mm. High-speed videos from a side view and a bottom view (Figure 1C) were acquired at 300 Hz using software FlyCapture (TELEDYNE FLIR). We trained DeepLabCut 39 to track the movement of tongue, jaw and nose (Figure 1I).\\n     8→\\n     9→Multi-regional electrophysiological recordings\\n    10→\\n    11→We designed a ﬂexible manipulator system for multiple Neuropixels probe insertions (https://www.janelia.org/open-science/manipulator-system-for-multiple-neuropixels-probe-recordings). Three to four small craniotomies (diameter, 1 \\u0018 1.5 mm) were made over target brain areas one day before the ﬁrst recording session. Details of recordings made with multiple Neuropixels probes in head-restrained behaving animals have been published (dx.doi.org/10.17504/protocols.io.8tphwmn). Target regions, insertions angles, probe types, etc. are summarized in Table S2. All coordinates are given with respect to Bregma (AP, anterior-posterior; ML, medial-lateral; DV, dorsal-ventral).\\n    12→\\n    13→Extracellular spikes were recorded using Neuropixels 1.0 probes, 2.0 single-shank (SS) and 2.0 multi-shank (MS) probes 3,4 (Table S2). Three to seven recordings were made from each craniotomy. Recording depth was inferred from manipulator readings (Sensapax uMp manipulators). After completion of probe insertion, brain tissue was allowed to settle for several minutes before recording. During recordings the craniotomies were immersed in saline.\\n    14→\\n    15→Probes were conﬁgured and data were visualized and streamed to disk using the open-source software package SpikeGLX (https://billkarsh.github.io/SpikeGLX/). Data from up to ﬁve simultaneously recorded probes were acquired at 30 kHz. Sync waves (0.5 s duty cycle TTL pulses) were recorded across probes and on auxiliary channels for synchronization.\\n    16→\\n    17→Photoinhibition\\n    18→\\n    19→Light from two 473 nm lasers (Coherent OBIS LS/LX) was used to illuminate the two hemispheres of ALM. The laser power was controlled by analog outputs controlled by a Bpod (Sanworks). Photoinactivation of ALM (centered on AP 2.5 mm; ML 1.5 mm) was performed through clear-skull cap implant11 or via craniotomy by directing the laser over the skull (beam diameter, 1.8 mm at 4 sigma). Photoinhibition of the left-, right-ALM hemisphere, or both hemispheres was deployed on a subset of ~25% randomly interleaved trials 11,21 (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice). To prevent the mice from distinguishing photostimulation trials from control rials using visual cues, a ‘masking ﬂash’ (1 ms pulses at 10 Hz) was delivered using 470 nm LEDs in front of the mice throughout the entire trial. For silencing ALM, we stimulated cortical GABAergic neurons in VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice. We used 40 Hz photostimulation with a sinusoidal temporal proﬁle (5 mW average power per hemisphere) and a 100 ms linear ramping down during the laser offset to reduce rebound neuronal activity. This photo stimulus is expected to silence glutamatergic neurons in all of ALM. 37 We silenced ALM activity during the late delay epoch (last 0.5 s), including the 100 ms ramp-down. Thus, photoinhibition always ended before the ‘Go’ cue.\\n    20→\\n    21→To analyze the effects of photoinhibition, units with at least 10 photostimulation trials were selected for analysis (Figures 5 and S5). Bilateral ALM photostimulation reduced behavior performance from 83.2% to 71.7% (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice, n = 93 sessions). To exclude the effects of rebound population activity in the thalamus 16 and other subcortical regions, we used a 20-120 ms\\n    22→time window after photo stimulation onset to measure the effect of ALM inactivation in downstream regions (Figures 5 and S5). For ALM itself, we used 500 ms (i.e. the entire inactivation period) to calculate spike rate reduction.\\n    23→\\n    24→Spike sorting and quality control\\n    25→\\n    26→We developed a data pipeline for preprocessing, spike sorting and quality control (https://github.com/jenniferColonell/ecephys_spike_sorting). The extracellular recording traces were ﬁrst band-pass ﬁltered (Butterworth acausal ﬁlter, 250 Hz - 9 kHz). We then sorted the dataset using both Kilosort2 32 (MATLAB version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/KS20_for_preprocessed_data: Release version of KS2 for preprocessed data) and Kilosort2.5 4 (Python version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/pykilosort: [WIP] Python port of Kilosort 2). Both sorting methods gave similar results. In the analyses presented in the paper, we used the output from Kilosort2. Each output cluster was associated with a list of 15 quality metrics calculated to assess isolation and sorting quality 7 (i.e. quality control) (https://github.com/AllenInstitute/ecephys_spike_sorting/tree/master/ecephys_spike_sorting/modules/quality_metrics).\\n    27→\\n    28→The details of the spike sorting and quality control are described in an accompanying white paper (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.v1). In brief, we used 15 cluster quality metrics to train classiﬁers - one for each of the major ﬁve brain areas (cortex, striatum, thalamus, midbrain and medulla). The classiﬁers were trained based on labels derived from manual curation. We curated Kilosort2 output (28 penetrations from ﬁve brain areas) using the open-source graphical user interface Phy (GitHub - cortex-lab/phy: phy: interactive visualization and manual spike sorting of large-scale ephys data). Each cluster was labeled as a ‘good’ or ‘unlabeled’ unit. Annotators were blind to location, recording condition and trial type information. Based on the annotated labels of all clusters and associated quality metrics, we trained ﬁve region-speciﬁc classiﬁers using logistic regression. Applying the trained classiﬁers to the rest of the dataset provided lists of units that were labeled as ‘good’ and were used for the analysis in this paper. We applied the cortex classiﬁer to ALM and other cortex, hippocampus, olfactory and cortical subplate units; the striatum classiﬁer to striatum and pallidum units; the thalamus classiﬁer to thalamus and hypothalamus units; the midbrain classiﬁer to midbrain and pons units; the medulla classiﬁer to medulla and cerebellum units. We calculated the rate at which the classiﬁer incorrectly labeled ’unlabeled’ units as ’good’, using 10-fold cross-validation on held-out manually labeled units (false alarm rate): 7.8%; striatum: 6.4%; thalamus: 7.3%; midbrain: 5.5%; medulla 4.3%. This method yielded 8717 good units from ALM, 7664 from striatum, 12808 from thalamus, 7495 from midbrain and 2928 from medulla, etc. Overall, the dataset consisted of 69,943 good units recorded across 173 behavioral sessions, from which 655 probe insertions were made. This corresponds to 25.9 % of clusters reported by Kilosort2. \\n    29→\\n\\n[metadata] {\\\"type\\\": \\\"text\\\", \\\"file\\\": {\\\"filePath\\\": \\\"/app/methods.txt\\\", \\\"content\\\": \\\"Behavior and video tracking\\\\n\\\\nMice performed an auditory delayed response task 21 (Figure 1A). The instruction stimuli during the sample epoch were pure tones played at one of two frequencies (3 kHz or 12 kHz). Each tone was played three times for 150 ms with 100 ms inter-tone intervals. The sample epoch was followed by a 1.2 s delay epoch. An auditory ‘Go’ cue (carrier frequency of 6 kHz with 360 Hz modulating fre-\\\\nquency, 0.1 s duration) indicated the end of the delay epoch. Licking early during the sample/delay epoch triggered a replay of the epoch. During the response epoch (answer period: 1.5 s) mice reported the instruction by licking one of the two lick ports. Licking (consumption period: 1.5 s) the correct lick port triggered a small water reward (0.1 ~ 0.2 ul). Licking the incorrect lick port triggered a timeout (1-3 s). After mice stopped licking for 1.5 s, the trial ended, followed by a 250 ms inter-trial-interval. Early lick trials and no\\\\nresponse trials were excluded for analysis. Overall performance was computed as the fraction of correct control trials (i.e. no photostimulation), excluding any early lick trials. We selected experimental sessions for analysis based on following criteria: overall behavioral performance (> 65%), and at least 50 correct lick left and lick right trials each. Mice performed on average 476 (Mean; range, 130-785) trials per session with 84% correct rate (range, 65-99%; STAR Methods). During the delay epoch, mice maintain a memory of their choice and plan a movement.\\\\n\\\\nTwo CMOS cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) were used to track orofacial movements of the mouse under IR light illumination (940 nm LED). The cameras were equipped with 4-12 mm focal length lenses (12VM412ASIR, Tamron) and a pixel resolution of 71 mm. High-speed videos from a side view and a bottom view (Figure 1C) were acquired at 300 Hz using software FlyCapture (TELEDYNE FLIR). We trained DeepLabCut 39 to track the movement of tongue, jaw and nose (Figure 1I).\\\\n\\\\nMulti-regional electrophysiological recordings\\\\n\\\\nWe designed a ﬂexible manipulator system for multiple Neuropixels probe insertions (https://www.janelia.org/open-science/manipulator-system-for-multiple-neuropixels-probe-recordings). Three to four small craniotomies (diameter, 1 \\\\u0018 1.5 mm) were made over target brain areas one day before the ﬁrst recording session. Details of recordings made with multiple Neuropixels probes in head-restrained behaving animals have been published (dx.doi.org/10.17504/protocols.io.8tphwmn). Target regions, insertions angles, probe types, etc. are summarized in Table S2. All coordinates are given with respect to Bregma (AP, anterior-posterior; ML, medial-lateral; DV, dorsal-ventral).\\\\n\\\\nExtracellular spikes were recorded using Neuropixels 1.0 probes, 2.0 single-shank (SS) and 2.0 multi-shank (MS) probes 3,4 (Table S2). Three to seven recordings were made from each craniotomy. Recording depth was inferred from manipulator readings (Sensapax uMp manipulators). After completion of probe insertion, brain tissue was allowed to settle for several minutes before recording. During recordings the craniotomies were immersed in saline.\\\\n\\\\nProbes were conﬁgured and data were visualized and streamed to disk using the open-source software package SpikeGLX (https://billkarsh.github.io/SpikeGLX/). Data from up to ﬁve simultaneously recorded probes were acquired at 30 kHz. Sync waves (0.5 s duty cycle TTL pulses) were recorded across probes and on auxiliary channels for synchronization.\\\\n\\\\nPhotoinhibition\\\\n\\\\nLight from two 473 nm lasers (Coherent OBIS LS/LX) was used to illuminate the two hemispheres of ALM. The laser power was controlled by analog outputs controlled by a Bpod (Sanworks). Photoinactivation of ALM (centered on AP 2.5 mm; ML 1.5 mm) was performed through clear-skull cap implant11 or via craniotomy by directing the laser over the skull (beam diameter, 1.8 mm at 4 sigma). Photoinhibition of the left-, right-ALM hemisphere, or both hemispheres was deployed on a subset of ~25% randomly interleaved trials 11,21 (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice). To prevent the mice from distinguishing photostimulation trials from control rials using visual cues, a ‘masking ﬂash’ (1 ms pulses at 10 Hz) was delivered using 470 nm LEDs in front of the mice throughout the entire trial. For silencing ALM, we stimulated cortical GABAergic neurons in VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice. We used 40 Hz photostimulation with a sinusoidal temporal proﬁle (5 mW average power per hemisphere) and a 100 ms linear ramping down during the laser offset to reduce rebound neuronal activity. This photo stimulus is expected to silence glutamatergic neurons in all of ALM. 37 We silenced ALM activity during the late delay epoch (last 0.5 s), including the 100 ms ramp-down. Thus, photoinhibition always ended before the ‘Go’ cue.\\\\n\\\\nTo analyze the effects of photoinhibition, units with at least 10 photostimulation trials were selected for analysis (Figures 5 and S5). Bilateral ALM photostimulation reduced behavior performance from 83.2% to 71.7% (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice, n = 93 sessions). To exclude the effects of rebound population activity in the thalamus 16 and other subcortical regions, we used a 20-120 ms\\\\ntime window after photo stimulation onset to measure the effect of ALM inactivation in downstream regions (Figures 5 and S5). For ALM itself, we used 500 ms (i.e. the entire inactivation period) to calculate spike rate reduction.\\\\n\\\\nSpike sorting and quality control\\\\n\\\\nWe developed a data pipeline for preprocessing, spike sorting and quality control (https://github.com/jenniferColonell/ecephys_spike_sorting). The extracellular recording traces were ﬁrst band-pass ﬁltered (Butterworth acausal ﬁlter, 250 Hz - 9 kHz). We then sorted the dataset using both Kilosort2 32 (MATLAB version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/KS20_for_preprocessed_data: Release version of KS2 for preprocessed data) and Kilosort2.5 4 (Python version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/pykilosort: [WIP] Python port of Kilosort 2). Both sorting methods gave similar results. In the analyses presented in the paper, we used the output from Kilosort2. Each output cluster was associated with a list of 15 quality metrics calculated to assess isolation and sorting quality 7 (i.e. quality control) (https://github.com/AllenInstitute/ecephys_spike_sorting/tree/master/ecephys_spike_sorting/modules/quality_metrics).\\\\n\\\\nThe details of the spike sorting and quality control are described in an accompanying white paper (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.v1). In brief, we used 15 cluster quality metrics to train classiﬁers - one for each of the major ﬁve brain areas (cortex, striatum, thalamus, midbrain and medulla). The classiﬁers were trained based on labels derived from manual curation. We curated Kilosort2 output (28 penetrations from ﬁve brain areas) using the open-source graphical user interface Phy (GitHub - cortex-lab/phy: phy: interactive visualization and manual spike sorting of large-scale ephys data). Each cluster was labeled as a ‘good’ or ‘unlabeled’ unit. Annotators were blind to location, recording condition and trial type information. Based on the annotated labels of all clusters and associated quality metrics, we trained ﬁve region-speciﬁc classiﬁers using logistic regression. Applying the trained classiﬁers to the rest of the dataset provided lists of units that were labeled as ‘good’ and were used for the analysis in this paper. We applied the cortex classiﬁer to ALM and other cortex, hippocampus, olfactory and cortical subplate units; the striatum classiﬁer to striatum and pallidum units; the thalamus classiﬁer to thalamus and hypothalamus units; the midbrain classiﬁer to midbrain and pons units; the medulla classiﬁer to medulla and cerebellum units. We calculated the rate at which the classiﬁer incorrectly labeled ’unlabeled’ units as ’good’, using 10-fold cross-validation on held-out manually labeled units (false alarm rate): 7.8%; striatum: 6.4%; thalamus: 7.3%; midbrain: 5.5%; medulla 4.3%. This method yielded 8717 good units from ALM, 7664 from striatum, 12808 from thalamus, 7495 from midbrain and 2928 from medulla, etc. Overall, the dataset consisted of 69,943 good units recorded across 173 behavioral sessions, from which 655 probe insertions were made. We selected experimental sessions for analysis based on following criteria: overall behavioral performance (> 65%), and at least 50 correct lick left and lick right trials each. Mice performed on average 476 (Mean; range, 130-785) trials per session with 84% correct rate (range, 65-99%; STAR Methods). During the delay epoch, mice maintain a memory of their choice and plan a movement.\\n\\nTwo CMOS cameras (CM3-U3-13Y3M, FLIR) were used to track orofacial movements of the mouse under IR light illumination (940 nm LED). The cameras were equipped with 4-12 mm focal length lenses (12VM412ASIR, Tamron) and a pixel resolution of 71 mm. High-speed videos from a side view and a bottom view (Figure 1C) were acquired at 300 Hz using software FlyCapture (TELEDYNE FLIR). We trained DeepLabCut 39 to track the movement of tongue, jaw and nose (Figure 1I).\\n\\nMulti-regional electrophysiological recordings\\n\\nWe designed a ﬂexible manipulator system for multiple Neuropixels probe insertions (https://www.janelia.org/open-science/manipulator-system-for-multiple-neuropixels-probe-recordings). Three to four small craniotomies (diameter, 1 \\u0018 1.5 mm) were made over target brain areas one day before the ﬁrst recording session. Details of recordings made with multiple Neuropixels probes in head-restrained behaving animals have been published (dx.doi.org/10.17504/protocols.io.8tphwmn). Target regions, insertions angles, probe types, etc. are summarized in Table S2. All coordinates are given with respect to Bregma (AP, anterior-posterior; ML, medial-lateral; DV, dorsal-ventral).\\n\\nExtracellular spikes were recorded using Neuropixels 1.0 probes, 2.0 single-shank (SS) and 2.0 multi-shank (MS) probes 3,4 (Table S2). Three to seven recordings were made from each craniotomy. Recording depth was inferred from manipulator readings (Sensapax uMp manipulators). After completion of probe insertion, brain tissue was allowed to settle for several minutes before recording. During recordings the craniotomies were immersed in saline.\\n\\nProbes were conﬁgured and data were visualized and streamed to disk using the open-source software package SpikeGLX (https://billkarsh.github.io/SpikeGLX/). Data from up to ﬁve simultaneously recorded probes were acquired at 30 kHz. Sync waves (0.5 s duty cycle TTL pulses) were recorded across probes and on auxiliary channels for synchronization.\\n\\nPhotoinhibition\\n\\nLight from two 473 nm lasers (Coherent OBIS LS/LX) was used to illuminate the two hemispheres of ALM. The laser power was controlled by analog outputs controlled by a Bpod (Sanworks). Photoinactivation of ALM (centered on AP 2.5 mm; ML 1.5 mm) was performed through clear-skull cap implant11 or via craniotomy by directing the laser over the skull (beam diameter, 1.8 mm at 4 sigma). Photoinhibition of the left-, right-ALM hemisphere, or both hemispheres was deployed on a subset of ~25% randomly interleaved trials 11,21 (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice). To prevent the mice from distinguishing photostimulation trials from control rials using visual cues, a ‘masking ﬂash’ (1 ms pulses at 10 Hz) was delivered using 470 nm LEDs in front of the mice throughout the entire trial. For silencing ALM, we stimulated cortical GABAergic neurons in VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice. We used 40 Hz photostimulation with a sinusoidal temporal proﬁle (5 mW average power per hemisphere) and a 100 ms linear ramping down during the laser offset to reduce rebound neuronal activity. This photo stimulus is expected to silence glutamatergic neurons in all of ALM. 37 We silenced ALM activity during the late delay epoch (last 0.5 s), including the 100 ms ramp-down. Thus, photoinhibition always ended before the ‘Go’ cue.\\n\\nTo analyze the effects of photoinhibition, units with at least 10 photostimulation trials were selected for analysis (Figures 5 and S5). Bilateral ALM photostimulation reduced behavior performance from 83.2% to 71.7% (N = 17 VGAT-ChR2-EYFP mice, n = 93 sessions). To exclude the effects of rebound population activity in the thalamus 16 and other subcortical regions, we used a 20-120 ms\\ntime window after photo stimulation onset to measure the effect of ALM inactivation in downstream regions (Figures 5 and S5). For ALM itself, we used 500 ms (i.e. the entire inactivation period) to calculate spike rate reduction.\\n\\nSpike sorting and quality control\\n\\nWe developed a data pipeline for preprocessing, spike sorting and quality control (https://github.com/jenniferColonell/ecephys_spike_sorting). The extracellular recording traces were ﬁrst band-pass ﬁltered (Butterworth acausal ﬁlter, 250 Hz - 9 kHz). We then sorted the dataset using both Kilosort2 32 (MATLAB version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/KS20_for_preprocessed_data: Release version of KS2 for preprocessed data) and Kilosort2.5 4 (Python version, GitHub - jenniferColonell/pykilosort: [WIP] Python port of Kilosort 2). Both sorting methods gave similar results. In the analyses presented in the paper, we used the output from Kilosort2. Each output cluster was associated with a list of 15 quality metrics calculated to assess isolation and sorting quality 7 (i.e. quality control) (https://github.com/AllenInstitute/ecephys_spike_sorting/tree/master/ecephys_spike_sorting/modules/quality_metrics).\\n\\nThe details of the spike sorting and quality control are described in an accompanying white paper (https://doi.org/10.25378/janelia.24066108.v1). In brief, we used 15 cluster quality metrics to train classiﬁers - one for each of the major ﬁve brain areas (cortex, striatum, thalamus, midbrain and medulla). The classiﬁers were trained based on labels derived from manual curation. We curated Kilosort2 output (28 penetrations from ﬁve brain areas) using the open-source graphical user interface Phy (GitHub - cortex-lab/phy: phy: interactive visualization and manual spike sorting of large-scale ephys data). Each cluster was labeled as a ‘good’ or ‘unlabeled’ unit. Annotators were blind to location, recording condition and trial type information. Based on the annotated labels of all clusters and associated quality metrics, we trained ﬁve region-speciﬁc classiﬁers using logistic regression. Applying the trained classiﬁers to the rest of the dataset provided lists of units that were labeled as ‘good’ and were used for the analysis in this paper. We applied the cortex classiﬁer to ALM and other cortex, hippocampus, olfactory and cortical subplate units; the striatum classiﬁer to striatum and pallidum units; the thalamus classiﬁer to thalamus and hypothalamus units; the midbrain classiﬁer to midbrain and pons units; the medulla classiﬁer to medulla and cerebellum units. We calculated the rate at which the classiﬁer incorrectly labeled ’unlabeled’ units as ’good’, using 10-fold cross-validation on held-out manually labeled units (false alarm rate): 7.8%; striatum: 6.4%; thalamus: 7.3%; midbrain: 5.5%; medulla 4.3%. This method yielded 8717 good units from ALM, 7664 from striatum, 12808 from thalamus, 7495 from midbrain and 2928 from medulla, etc. Overall, the dataset consisted of 69,943 good units recorded across 173 behavioral sessions, from which 655 probe insertions were made. This corresponds to 25.9 % of clusters reported by Kilosort2. \\n\",\n   496→              \"numLines\": 29,\n   497→              \"startLine\": 1,\n   498→              \"totalLines\": 29\n   499→            }\n   500→          },\n   501→          \"raw_tool_result\": {\n   502→            \"tool_use_id\": \"toolu_013Y8zQa5A8MLswZiSV1Yb9r\",\n   503→            \"type\": \"tool_result\",\n   504→            \"content\": \"     1→Behavior and video tracking\\n     2→\\n     3→Mice performed an auditory delayed response task 21 (Figure 1A). The instruction stimuli during the sample epoch were pure tones played at one of two frequencies (3 kHz or 12 kHz). Each tone was played three times for 150 ms with 100 ms inter-tone intervals. The sample epoch was followed by a 1.2 s delay epoch. An auditory ‘Go’ cue (carrier frequency of 6 kHz with 360 Hz modulating fre-\\n     4→quency, 0.1 s duration) indicated the end of the delay epoch. Licking early during the sample/delay epoch triggered a replay of the epoch. During the response epoch (answer period: 1.5 s) mice reported the instruction by licking one of the two lick ports. Licking (consumption period: 1.5 s) the correct lick port triggered a small water reward (0.1 ~ 0.2 ul). Licking the incorrect lick port triggered a timeout (1-3 s). After mice stopped licking for 1.5 s, the trial ended, followed by a 250 ms inter-trial-interval. Early lick trials and no\\n     5→response trials were excluded for analysis. Overall performance was computed as the fraction of correct control trials (i.e. no photostimulation), excluding any early lick trials. We selected experimental sessions for analysis based on following criteria: overall behavioral performance (> 65%), and at least 50 correct lick left and lick right trials each.     'Infracerebellar nucleus',\\\\n    ],\\\\n    'Cerebellum': [\\\\n        'Cerebellum',\\\\n        'Lobule',\\\\n        'Lobules',\\\\n        'Simple lobule',\\\\n        'Copula pyramidis',\\\\n        'Interposed nucleus',\\\\n        'Fastigial nucleus',\\\\n        'Declive',\\\\n        'Paramedian lobule',\\\\n        'Crus',\\\\n        'Nodulus',\\\\n        'Uvula',\\\\n        'Pyramus',\\\\n        'Lingula',\\\\n    ],\\\\n    'Olfactory': [\\\\n        'Anterior olfactory nucleus',\\\\n        'Piriform area',\\\\n        'Olfactory areas',\\\\n        'Olfactory tubercle',\\\\n        'Taenia tecta',\\\\n        'Accessory olfactory bulb',\\\\n        'Endopiriform nucleus',\\\\n    ],\\\\n    'CorticalSubplate': [\\\\n        'Cortical septal nucleus',\\\\n        'Septofimbrial nucleus',\\\\n        'Triangular nucleus of septum',\\\\n    ],\\\\n}\\\\n\\\\n\\\\ndef map_anno_name_to_region(anno_name):\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Map a detailed Allen Brain Atlas annotation name to a high-level region category.\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n    if not anno_name or anno_name.strip() == '':\\\\n        return None\\\\n    for region, prefixes in REGION_MAPPING.items():\\\\n        for prefix in prefixes:\\\\n            if anno_name.startswith(prefix) or anno_name.startswith(prefix + ',') or anno_name.startswith(prefix + '/'):\\\\n                return region\\\\n    return None\\\\n\\\\n\\\\ndef compute_firing_rates(spike_times, go_cue_time, bin_edges):\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Compute firing rates for a single unit in a single trial.\\\\n\\\\n    Args:\\\\n        spike_times: array of spike times (absolute)\\\\n        go_cue_time: go cue time (absolute)\\\\n        bin_edges: bin edges relative to go cue\\\\n\\\\n    Returns:\\\\n        firing_rates: array of firing rates (Hz), shape (n_bins,)\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n    # Align spike times to go cue\\\\n    rel_times = spike_times - go_cue_time\\\\n    # Only consider spikes in the window\\\\n    mask = (rel_times >= bin_edges[0]) & (rel_times < bin_edges[-1])\\\\n    rel_times = rel_times[mask]\\\\n    # Count spikes in each bin\\\\n    counts, _ = np.histogram(rel_times, bins=bin_edges)\\\\n    # Convert to firing rates\\\\n    bin_width = bin_edges[1] - bin_edges[0]\\\\n    return counts.astype(np.float64) / bin_width\\\\n\\\\n\\\\ndef get_tone_onset_relative_to_go(sample_start_times, go_cue_time):\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Find the tone onset time relative to go cue for a single trial.\\\\n\\\\n    The tone onset is the last sample_start before the go cue\\\\n    (accounts for early lick replays).\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n    before = sample_start_times[sample_start_times < go_cue_time]\\\\n    if len(before) > 0:\\\\n        return before[-1] - go_cue_time  # negative value\\\\n    return None\\\\n\\\\n\\\\ndef extract_tongue_y_for_trial(tongue_timestamps, tongue_y, tongue_likelihood,\\\\n                                go_cue_time, bin_edges, likelihood_thresh=0.9):\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Extract tongue y-position in time bins for a single trial.\\\\n\\\\n    Returns:\\\\n        tongue_y_binned: array of mean y-positions per bin, shape (n_bins,)\\\\n            NaN for bins with no high-confidence tracking data\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n    n_bins = len(bin_edges) - 1\\\\n    tongue_y_binned = np.full(n_bins, np.nan)\\\\n\\\\n    # Get absolute time window\\\\n    t_abs_start = go_cue_time + bin_edges[0]\\\\n    t_abs_end = go_cue_time + bin_edges[-1]\\\\n\\\\n    # Find tongue data in this window\\\\n    idx = np.searchsorted(tongue_timestamps, [t_abs_start, t_abs_end])\\\\n    ts_window = tongue_timestamps[idx[0]:idx[1]]\\\\n    y_window = tongue_y[idx[0]:idx[1]]\\\\n    like_window = tongue_likelihood[idx[0]:idx[1]]\\\\n\\\\n    if len(ts_window) == 0:\\\\n        return tongue_y_binned\\\\n\\\\n    # Align to go cue\\\\n    ts_rel = ts_window - go_cue_time\\\\n\\\\n    # For each bin, compute mean y from high-confidence points\\\\n    for b in range(n_bins):\\\\n        bin_mask = (ts_rel >= bin_edges[b]) & (ts_rel < bin_edges[b + 1])\\\\n        if not np.any(bin_mask):\\\\n            continue\\\\n        conf_mask = bin_mask & (like_window >= likelihood_thresh)\\\\n        if np.any(conf_mask):\\\\n            tongue_y_binned[b] = np.mean(y_window[conf_mask])\\\\n\\\\n    return tongue_y_binned\\\\n\\\\n\\\\ndef process_session(nwb_path, verbose=True):\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"Process a single NWB session file.\\\\n\\\\n    Returns:\\\\n        session_data: dict with neural, input, output, metadata for this session,\\\\n                      or None if session doesn't meet criteria\\\\n    \\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\"\\\\n    if verbose:\\\\n        print(f\\\\\\\"Processing: {os.path.basename(nwb_path)}\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n    f = h5py.File(nwb_path, 'r')\\\\n\\\\n    try:\\\\n        # === Extract trial information ===\\\\n        n_trials = len(f['intervals/trials/id'][:])\\\\n        outcomes = np.array([o.decode() if isinstance(o, bytes) else o\\\\n                            for o in f['intervals/trials/outcome'][:]])\\\\n        early_lick = np.array([e.decode() if isinstance(e, bytes) else e\\\\n                              for e in f['intervals/trials/early_lick'][:]])\\\\n        instructions = np.array([i.decode() if isinstance(i, bytes) else i\\\\n                                for i in f['intervals/trials/trial_instruction'][:]])\\\\n        auto_water = f['intervals/trials/auto_water'][:]\\\\n        free_water = f['intervals/trials/free_water'][:]\\\\n\\\\n        # Photostim info from trial table\\\\n        ps_onset_str = np.array([p.decode() if isinstance(p, bytes) else p\\\\n                                for p in f['intervals/trials/photostim_onset'][:]])\\\\n        ps_dur_str = np.array([p.decode() if isinstance(p, bytes) else p\\\\n                              for p in f['intervals/trials/photostim_duration'][:]])\\\\n\\\\n        # Go cue times\\\\n        go_cue_times = f['acquisition/BehavioralEvents/go_start_times/timestamps'][:]\\\\n\\\\n        # Sample start times (for tone onset calculation)\\\\n        sample_start_times = f['acquisition/BehavioralEvents/sample_start_times/timestamps'][:]\\\\n\\\\n        assert len(go_cue_times) == n_trials, f\\\\\\\"Go cue count ({len(go_cue_times)}) != trial count ({n_trials})\\\\\\\"\\\\n\\\\n        # === Load units ===\\\\n        classification = np.array([c.decode() if isinstance(c, bytes) else c\\\\n                                   for c in f['units/classification'][:]])\\\\n        anno_names = np.array([a.decode() if isinstance(a, bytes) else a\\\\n                              for a in f['units/anno_name'][:]])\\\\n\\\\n        # Filter by quality (classifier-based QC)\\\\n        good_mask = classification == 'good'\\\\n\\\\n        # Map brain regions and filter units with valid regions\\\\n        unit_regions = []\\\\n        unit_indices = []\\\\n        for i in range(len(classification)):\\\\n            if not good_mask[i]:\\\\n                continue\\\\n            region = map_anno_name_to_region(anno_names[i])\\\\n            if region is not None:\\\\n                unit_regions.append(region)\\\\n                unit_indices.append(i)\\\\n\\\\n        if len(unit_indices) == 0:\\\\n            if verbose:\\\\n                print(f\\\\\\\"  Skipping: no good units with valid regions\\\\\\\")\\\\n            return None\\\\n\\\\n        unit_indices = np.array(unit_indices)\\\\n        n_units = len(unit_indices)\\\\n\\\\n        # === Determine valid trials based on recording observation window ===\\\\n        # Units may only have data for a subset of trials if the recording\\\\n        # didn't span the entire behavioral session\\\\n        obs_intervals = f['units/obs_intervals'][:]\\\\n        obs_intervals_index = f['units/obs_intervals_index'][:]\\\\n\\\\n        # Find the minimum observation end time across all selected units\\\\n        min_obs_end = np.inf\\\\n        max_obs_start = -np.inf\\\\n        for ui in unit_indices:\\\\n            oi_start = 0 if ui == 0 else obs_intervals_index[ui - 1]\\\\n            oi_end = obs_intervals_index[ui]\\\\n            unit_obs = obs_intervals[oi_start:oi_end]\\\\n            if len(unit_obs) > 0:\\\\n                min_obs_end = min(min_obs_end, unit_obs[-1, 1])\\\\n                max_obs_start = max(max_obs_start, unit_obs[0, 0])\\\\n\\\\n        # Only include trials where the go cue (and surrounding window) falls\\\\n        # within the recording observation window\\\\n        recording_valid = ((go_cue_times + T_START) >= max_obs_start - 0.1) & \\\\\\\\\\\\n                          ((go_cue_times + T_END) <= min_obs_end + 0.1)\\\\n\\\\n        if verbose and np.sum(~recording_valid) > 0:\\\\n            print(f\\\\\\\"  Recording window limits: {np.sum(recording_valid)}/{n_trials} trials have neural data\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n        # === Session filtering ===\\\\n        # Use ALL trials for session-level performance calculation (as in the paper)\\\\n        # Control trials: no photostim, no early lick, no auto_water, no free_water\\\\n        is_control = ((early_lick == 'no early') &\\\\n                      (auto_water == 0) &\\\\n                      (free_water == 0) &\\\\n                      (ps_onset_str == 'N/A'))\\\\n\\\\n        control_hits_left = np.sum(is_control & (outcomes == 'hit') & (instructions == 'left'))\\\\n        control_hits_right = np.sum(is_control & (outcomes == 'hit') & (instructions == 'right'))\\\\n\\\\n        # Performance on control trials (excluding ignore/no-response)\\\\n        control_responding = is_control & (outcomes != 'ignore')\\\\n        n_control_responding = np.sum(control_responding)\\\\n        if n_control_responding == 0:\\\\n            if verbose:\\\\n                print(f\\\\\\\"  Skipping: no responding control trials\\\\\\\")\\\\n            return None\\\\n\\\\n        performance = np.sum(is_control & (outcomes == 'hit')) / n_control_responding\\\\n\\\\n        if verbose:\\\\n            print(f\\\\\\\"  Performance: {performance:.1%}, Correct L/R: {control_hits_left}/{control_hits_right}\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n        if performance <= MIN_PERFORMANCE:\\\\n            if verbose:\\\\n                print(f\\\\\\\"  Skipping: performance {performance:.1%} <= {MIN_PERFORMANCE:.0%}\\\\\\\")\\\\n            return None\\\\n\\\\n        if control_hits_left < MIN_CORRECT_PER_DIRECTION or control_hits_right < MIN_CORRECT_PER_DIRECTION:\\\\n            if verbose:\\\\n                print(f\\\\\\\"  Skipping: insufficient correct trials (L={control_hits_left}, R={control_hits_right})\\\\\\\")\\\\n            return None\\\\n\\\\n        # === Select trials ===\\\\n        # Exclude auto_water and free_water trials (as in reference code)\\\\n        # Also must have valid neural recording data\\\\n        trial_mask = (auto_water == 0) & (free_water == 0) & recording_valid\\\\n        trial_indices = np.where(trial_mask)[0]\\\\n\\\\n        if len(trial_indices) < 2:\\\\n            if verbose:\\\\n                print(f\\\\\\\"  Skipping: fewer than 2 valid trials\\\\\\\")\\\\n            return None\\\\n\\\\n        if verbose:\\\\n            print(f\\\\\\\"  Good units: {n_units}\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n        # === Load spike times ===\\\\n        spike_times_data = f['units/spike_times'][:]\\\\n        spike_times_index = f['units/spike_times_index'][:]\\\\n\\\\n        # Build per-unit spike time arrays\\\\n        unit_spike_times = []\\\\n        for ui in unit_indices:\\\\n            start_idx = 0 if ui == 0 else spike_times_index[ui - 1]\\\\n            end_idx = spike_times_index[ui]\\\\n            unit_spike_times.append(spike_times_data[start_idx:end_idx])\\\\n\\\\n        # === Load tongue tracking ===\\\\n        tongue_ts = f['acquisition/BehavioralTimeSeries/Camera0_side_TongueTracking/timestamps'][:]\\\\n        tongue_data = f['acquisition/BehavioralTimeSeries/Camera0_side_TongueTracking/data'][:]\\\\n        tongue_y = tongue_data[:, 1]  # y-position\\\\n        tongue_like = tongue_data[:, 2]  # likelihood/confidence\\\\n\\\\n        # === Process each trial ===\\\\n        neural_trials = []\\\\n        input_trials = []\\\\n        output_choice = []\\\\n        output_outcome = []\\\\n        output_early_lick = []\\\\n        tongue_y_all_trials = []\\\\n\\\\n        for ti in trial_indices:\\\\n            gc = go_cue_times[ti]\\\\n\\\\n            # --- Neural data: firing rates ---\\\\n            fr_matrix = np.zeros((n_units, N_BINS), dtype=np.float64)\\\\n            for j, st in enumerate(unit_spike_times):\\\\n                fr_matrix[j, :] = compute_firing_rates(st, gc, BIN_EDGES)\\\\n            neural_trials.append(fr_matrix)\\\\n\\\\n            # --- Input: time from tone onset ---\\\\n            tone_onset_rel = get_tone_onset_relative_to_go(sample_start_times, gc)\\\\n            if tone_onset_rel is None:\\\\n                # Fallback: use typical value\\\\n                tone_onset_rel = -1.85\\\\n            time_from_tone = BIN_CENTERS - tone_onset_rel  # time since tone onset at each bin\\\\n\\\\n            # --- Input: photostim on/off ---\\\\n            photostim_binary = np.zeros(N_BINS, dtype=np.float64)\\\\n            if ps_onset_str[ti] != 'N/A':\\\\n                try:\\\\n                    ps_onset_abs = float(ps_onset_str[ti])\\\\n                    ps_dur = float(ps_dur_str[ti])\\\\n                    # Convert to relative to go cue\\\\n                    ps_start_rel = ps_onset_abs - gc\\\\n                    ps_end_rel = ps_start_rel + ps_dur\\\\n                    # Mark bins where photostim is on\\\\n                    photostim_binary = ((BIN_CENTERS >= ps_start_rel) &\\\\n                                       (BIN_CENTERS < ps_end_rel)).astype(np.float64)\\\\n                except (ValueError, TypeError):\\\\n                    pass\\\\n\\\\n            input_trial = np.stack([time_from_tone, photostim_binary], axis=0)  # (2, n_bins)\\\\n            input_trials.append(input_trial)\\\\n\\\\n            # --- Output: choice ---\\\\n            if instructions[ti] == 'left':\\\\n                choice = 0\\\\n            else:\\\\n                choice = 1\\\\n            output_choice.append(choice)\\\\n\\\\n            # --- Output: outcome ---\\\\n            if outcomes[ti] == 'ignore':\\\\n                outcome = 0\\\\n            elif outcomes[ti] == 'miss':\\\\n                outcome = 1\\\\n            else:  # hit\\\\n                outcome = 2\\\\n            output_outcome.append(outcome)\\\\n\\\\n            # --- Output: early lick ---\\\\n            el = 1 if early_lick[ti] == 'early' else 0\\\\n            output_early_lick.append(el)\\\\n\\\\n            # --- Tongue y-position (continuous, will discretize later) ---\\\\n            ty = extract_tongue_y_for_trial(tongue_ts, tongue_y, tongue_like,\\\\n                                           gc, BIN_EDGES, TONGUE_LIKELIHOOD_THRESH)\\\\n            tongue_y_all_trials.append(ty)\\\\n\\\\n        # === Discretize tongue y-position per session ===\\\\n        # Collect all valid (non-NaN) tongue y values across all trials in this session\\\\n        all_tongue_y_vals = np.concatenate(tongue_y_all_trials)\\\\n        valid_tongue_y = all_tongue_y_vals[~np.isnan(all_tongue_y_vals)]\\\\n\\\\n        if len(valid_tongue_y) > 0:\\\\n            p40 = np.percentile(valid_tongue_y, 40)\\\\n            p60 = np.percentile(valid_tongue_y, 60)\\\\n\\\\n            tongue_y_discrete_trials = []\\\\n            for ty in tongue_y_all_trials:\\\\n                ty_disc = np.ones(N_BINS, dtype=np.int64)  # default to middle category\\\\n                valid_mask = ~np.isnan(ty)\\\\n                ty_disc[valid_mask & (ty < p40)] = 0\\\\n                ty_disc[valid_mask & (ty >= p40) & (ty <= p60)] = 1\\\\n                ty_disc[valid_mask & (ty > p60)] = 2\\\\n                tongue_y_discrete_trials.append(ty_disc)\\\\n        else:\\\\n            # No valid tongue tracking - all middle category\\\\n            tongue_y_discrete_trials = [np.ones(N_BINS, dtype=np.int64) for _ in range(len(trial_indices))]\\\\n\\\\n        # === Build output arrays ===\\\\n        output_trials = []\\\\n        for i in range(len(trial_indices)):\\\\n            out = np.array([\\\\n                np.full(N_BINS, output_choice[i]),       # choice (per-trial, broadcast)\\\\n                np.full(N_BINS, output_outcome[i]),      # outcome (per-trial, broadcast)\\\\n                np.full(N_BINS, output_early_lick[i]),   # early lick (per-trial, broadcast)\\\\n                tongue_y_discrete_trials[i],              # tongue y (time-varying)\\\\n            ], dtype=np.float64)  # (4, n_bins)\\\\n            output_trials.append(out)\\\\n\\\\n        # Extract subject ID from path\\\\n        basename = os.path.basename(nwb_path)\\\\n        subject_id = basename.split('_ses-')[0].replace('sub-', '')\\\\n\\\\n        if verbose:\\\\n            print(f\\\\\\\"  Kept {len(trial_indices)} trials, {n_units} neurons\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n        return {\\\\n            'neural': neural_trials,\\\\n            'input': input_trials,\\\\n            'output': output_trials,\\\\n            'subject_id': subject_id,\\\\n            'unit_regions': unit_regions,\\\\n            'session_file': basename,\\\\n        }\\\\n\\\\n    finally:\\\\n        f.close()\\\\n\\\\n\\\\ndef main():\\\\n    parser = argparse.ArgumentParser(description='Convert NWB data to decoder format')\\\\n    parser.add_argument('--sample', action='store_true', help='Only process first 5 sessions')\\\\n    parser.add_argument('--output', type=str, default='converted_data.pkl', help='Output file path')\\\\n    args = parser.parse_args()\\\\n\\\\n    # Find all NWB files\\\\n    nwb_files = sorted(glob.glob('data/sub-*/sub-*.nwb'))\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"Found {len(nwb_files)} NWB files\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n    if args.sample:\\\\n        nwb_files = nwb_files[:5]\\\\n        print(f\\\\\\\"Sample mode: processing first {len(nwb_files)} files\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n    # Process all sessions\\\\n    all_neural = []\\\\n    all_input = []\\\\n    all_output = []\\\\n    all_subject_ids = []\\\\n    all_unit_regions = []\\\\n    all_session_files = []\\\\n\\\\n    n_skipped = 0\\\\n    n_processed = 0\\\\n\\\\n    for nwb_path in nwb_files:\\\\n        result = process_session(nwb_path)\\\\n        if result is None:\\\\n            n_skipped += 1\\\\n            continue\\\\n\\\\n        all_neural.append(result['neural'])\\\\n        all_input.append(result['input'])\\\\n        all_output.append(result['output'])\\\\n        all_subject_ids.append(result['subject_id'])\\\\n        all_unit_regions.append(result['unit_regions'])\\\\n        all_session_files.append(result['session_file'])\\\\n        n_processed += 1\\\\n\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\\nProcessed: {n_processed} sessions, Skipped: {n_skipped}\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n    # Build subjects list and index\\\\n    unique_subjects = sorted(set(all_subject_ids))\\\\n    subject_idx = np.array([unique_subjects.index(s) for s in all_subject_ids])\\\\n\\\\n    # Build brain_regions list and index\\\\n    all_region_names = set()\\\\n    for regions in all_unit_regions:\\\\n        all_region_names.update(regions)\\\\n    brain_regions = sorted(all_region_names)\\\\n\\\\n    brain_region_idx = []\\\\n    for regions in all_unit_regions:\\\\n        idx = np.array([brain_regions.index(r) for r in regions])\\\\n        brain_region_idx.append(idx)\\\\n\\\\n    # Build final data structure\\\\n    data = {\\\\n        'neural': all_neural,\\\\n        'input': all_input,\\\\n        'output': all_output,\\\\n\\\\n        'subjects': unique_subjects,\\\\n        'subject_idx': subject_idx,\\\\n\\\\n        'brain_regions': brain_regions,\\\\n        'brain_region_idx': brain_region_idx,\\\\n\\\\n        'input_names': ['time_from_tone_onset', 'photostim_on'],\\\\n        'output_names': ['choice', 'outcome', 'early_lick', 'tongue_y_position'],\\\\n        'output_values': [\\\\n            ['left', 'right'],           # choice: 0=left, 1=right\\\\n            ['ignore', 'miss', 'hit'],   # outcome: 0=ignore, 1=miss, 2=hit\\\\n            ['no', 'yes'],               # early_lick: 0=no, 1=yes\\\\n            ['low', 'middle', 'high'],   # tongue_y: 0=<p40, 1=p40-p60, 2=>p60\\\\n        ],\\\\n\\\\n        'metadata': {\\\\n            'task_description': 'Auditory delayed response task: mice heard tones (3kHz or 12kHz) during sample epoch, maintained memory during delay, then reported by licking left or right port after go cue.',\\\\n            'time_bin_size': BIN_WIDTH * 1000,  # in ms\\\\n            'temporal_alignment_event': 'Go cue onset',\\\\n            'off_start': T_START,\\\\n            'off_end': T_END,\\\\n            'n_sessions': n_processed,\\\\n            'n_subjects': len(unique_subjects),\\\\n            'session_files': all_session_files,\\\\n            'neuron_qc': 'classifier-based quality control (classification=good)',\\\\n            'session_filter': f'Performance > {MIN_PERFORMANCE:.0%}, >= {MIN_CORRECT_PER_DIRECTION} correct trials per direction on control trials',\\\\n            'trial_filter': 'Excluded auto_water and free_water trials',\\\\n            'firing_rate_method': f'{BIN_WIDTH*1000:.0f}ms non-overlapping bins, spike count / bin_width',\\\\n            'tongue_tracking': f'DeepLabCut side-view tongue y-position, likelihood threshold {TONGUE_LIKELIHOOD_THRESH}',\\\\n        },\\\\n    }\\\\n\\\\n    # Print summary statistics\\\\n    total_neurons = sum(len(r) for r in brain_region_idx)\\\\n    total_trials = sum(len(s) for s in all_neural)\\\\n    neurons_per_session = [all_neural[i][0].shape[0] for i in range(len(all_neural))]\\\\n    trials_per_session = [len(s) for s in all_neural]\\\\n\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\\n=== Dataset Summary ===\\\\\\\")\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"Sessions: {n_processed}\\\\\\\")\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"Subjects: {len(unique_subjects)}\\\\\\\")\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"Total neurons (across sessions): {total_neurons}\\\\\\\")\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"Total trials: {total_trials}\\\\\\\")\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"Neurons per session: mean={np.mean(neurons_per_session):.1f}, \\\\\\\"\\\\n          f\\\\\\\"range=[{np.min(neurons_per_session)}, {np.max(neurons_per_session)}]\\\\\\\")\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"Trials per session: mean={np.mean(trials_per_session):.1f}, \\\\\\\"\\\\n          f\\\\\\\"range=[{np.min(trials_per_session)}, {np.max(trials_per_session)}]\\\\\\\")\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"Time bins: {N_BINS} ({BIN_WIDTH*1000:.0f}ms each)\\\\\\\")\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"Time window: [{T_START}, {T_END}]s relative to go cue\\\\\\\")\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"Brain regions: {brain_regions}\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n    # Region distribution\\\\n    region_counts = {}\\\\n    for regions in all_unit_regions:\\\\n        for r in regions:\\\\n            region_counts[r] = region_counts.get(r, 0) + 1\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\\nNeurons per region:\\\\\\\")\\\\n    for r in sorted(region_counts, key=lambda x: -region_counts[x]):\\\\n        print(f\\\\\\\"  {r}: {region_counts[r]}\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n    # Save\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"\\\\\\\\nSaving to {args.output}...\\\\\\\")\\\\n    with open(args.output, 'wb') as f:\\\\n        pickle.dump(data, f, protocol=4)\\\\n\\\\n    file_size = os.path.getsize(args.output)\\\\n    print(f\\\\\\\"Saved ({file_size / 1e9:.2f} GB)\\\\\\\")\\\\n\\\\n    return data\\\\n\\\\n\\\\nif __name__ == '__main__':\\\\n    main()\\\\n\\\", \\\"structuredPatch\\\": [{\\\"oldStart\\\": 571, \\\"oldLines\\\": 11, \\\"newStart\\\": 571, \\\"newLines\\\": 11, \\\"lines\\\": [\\\"         output_trials =
